}}=-(ig)K\int\dfrac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\dfrac{i}{k_{+}}

at zero external momentum. Wick rotating takes the propagator factor
k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\pm}^{E}=ik_{0}^{E}\pm k_{1}
with
k_{0}=ik_{0}^{E}
. Then, the Euclidean integral takes the form

\displaystyle(ig)Ki\int\dfrac{d^{2}k^{E}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\dfrac{i}{k^{E}_{+}}

with
k^{E}_{+}\in\mathbb{C}
such that the integral is over the the whole complex plane. This vanishes as a corollary of

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{C}}d^{2}z\,z^{k}=0

(110)

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
.

The two diagrams differ by a minus sign because of the orientation of the fermion arrows and the two contributions cancel. As expected, the vertex is explicitly not renormalised to one-loop order.

It is also worth noting that Lorentz symmetry prohibits four-fermi terms such as
|\tilde{\mathcal{B}}|^{4}
. In terms of diagrams, the one loop corrections to such a vertex have the form
\int\dfrac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{k_{-}^{2}}
which, after Wick rotating, vanish following (
110
), as required. Any fermion loop that contains only left-moving or only right-moving fermions must necessarily vanish.

Chiral Gross-Neveu Model

The chiral Gross-Neveu model consists of
N
Dirac fermions
\Psi^{i}
interacting through a four-fermi term. In the chiral basis, we write the Dirac fermions in terms of their Weyl fermions as
\Psi^{i}=(\rho^{i},\tilde{\chi}^{i})^{T}
such that the interaction term is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L_{\chi\text{GN}}}=\dfrac{g^{2}}{2}\left[(\bar{\Psi}_{i}\Psi^{i})^{2}-(\bar{\Psi}_{i}\gamma^{5}\Psi^{i})^{2}\right]=g^{2}(\tilde{\chi}^{\dagger}_{i}\rho^{i})(\rho^{\dagger}_{j}\tilde{\chi}^{j})

Crucially, the flavour indices mix between the left and right-moving fermions. We now reproduce the famous large
N
asymptotic freedom of this model while working with Weyl fermions. This will serve as a nice warm-up for the more involved calculations below. We use the same conventions as above with solid lines for
\rho
and dotted lines for
\tilde{\chi}
.

As above, any mass corrections vanish. The only one-loop vertex contribution at large
N
is

\displaystyle\Gamma=\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to105.39pt{\vbox to55.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-35.5842pt\lower-88.51299pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.75pt,1.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 91.32 -83.71 C 94.6 -68.02 105.08 -59.48 116.6 -57.86 C 130.96 -55.85 146.92 -64.6 152.5 -83.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 124.55 -55.27 C 122.17 -56.49 119.97 -57.22 117.96 -57.46 C 119.98 -57.59 122.21 -58.19 124.67 -59.27}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 91.32 -83.71 C 97.79 -120.24 145.55 -121.01 152.21 -84.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 117.28 -113.52 C 119.66 -112.29 121.85 -111.55 123.86 -111.3 C 121.84 -111.19 119.6 -110.59 117.14 -109.52}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}=-(ig^{2})^{2}N\int\dfrac{d^{2}k}{(2\pi)^{2}}\dfrac{i}{k_{+}}\dfrac{i}{k_{-}}=-ig^{4}N\int\dfrac{d^{2}k_{E}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\dfrac{1}{-k_{E}^{2}}

at vanishing external momentum and we Wick rotate for the final equality. The momentum integral can be calculated using dimensional regularisation. Working in
d=2-\epsilon
dimensions, it reads

\displaystyle\int\dfrac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\dfrac{1}{k^{2}}=\dfrac{\Gamma\left(1-\dfrac{d}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}=\dfrac{1}{4\pi}\left(\dfrac{2}{\epsilon}\right)+\text{regular in }\epsilon

(111)

Then writing the dimensionless coupling as
g^{2}=g^{2}(\mu)\mu^{\epsilon}
results in the four-point function

\displaystyle\Gamma\sim\dfrac{iNg^{4}}{4\pi}\left(\dfrac{2}{\epsilon}\right)\left(1+\epsilon\log(\mu^{2})\right)

where we ignore terms regular in
\epsilon
. In the MS-scheme, we choose the counterterm
i\delta g^{2}=\frac{-iNg^{4}(\mu)}{(2\pi\epsilon)}
to cancel the pole which leads to the effective vertex

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\text{eff}}(\mu)=ig^{2}(\mu)+\dfrac{iNg^{4}(\mu)}{2\pi}\log(\mu^{2})

Finally the condition
\mu\dfrac{\partial g^{2}_{\text{eff}}}{\partial\mu}=0
gives the beta function

\displaystyle\beta(g^{2})=\dfrac{-N(g^{2})^{2}}{\pi}

Happily, this matches the famous original result of Gross and Neveu
gn
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}^{\text{IR}}_{\text{4}}=&g_{1}\operatorname{tr}(\lambda_{S}^{\dagger}\lambda_{S})\operatorname{tr}(\tilde{\lambda}_{B}^{\dagger}\tilde{\lambda}_{B})+g_{2}\operatorname{tr}(\lambda_{S}^{\dagger}\lambda_{S})(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathcal{B}})+g_{3}\operatorname{tr}(\lambda_{A}^{\dagger}\lambda_{A})\operatorname{tr}(\tilde{\lambda}_{B}^{\dagger}\tilde{\lambda}_{B})\\
+&g_{4}\operatorname{tr}(\lambda_{A}^{\dagger}\lambda_{A})(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathcal{B}})+g_{5}(\lambda^{{\dagger}}_{S\,ab}\lambda_{S}^{bc})(\tilde{\lambda}^{{\dagger}}_{B\,cI}\tilde{\lambda}_{B}^{aI})+g_{6}(\lambda^{{\dagger}}_{A\,IJ}\lambda_{A}^{JK})(\tilde{\lambda}^{{\dagger}}_{B\,aK}\tilde{\lambda}_{B}^{aI})\\
+&g_{7}\lambda_{A}^{IJ}\lambda_{S}^{ab}\tilde{\lambda}^{{\dagger}}_{B\,aI}\tilde{\lambda}^{{\dagger}}_{B\,bJ}+\text{h.c.}\end{aligned}

We can now compute the one-loop corrections. There are a number of observations that significantly simplify the calculation. First, as we saw for the
p=0
case above, there are no propagator corrections. Second, any fermion loop containing a fermion of single chirality (i.e. a loop with only a left-moving fermion) is identically zero as a corollary of (
110
). Finally, any vertex correction to interactions of the form
\operatorname{tr}(\text{LH}\times\text{LH})\operatorname{tr}(\text{RH}\times\text{RH})
, for left/right-handed fermions with the trace over the flavour indices, is zero. This is due to a similar cancellation as in the
p=0
case above where contributions from two diagrams of opposing internal fermion arrows exactly cancel. This means that
g_{1},...,g_{4}
receive no one-loop corrections and only operators that mix flavour get modified.

Nontrivial corrections are due to diagrams that mix flavour, such as

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 115.82 -55.08 C 118.25 -55.08 120.33 -55.4 122.06 -56.04 C 120.47 -55.1 118.92 -53.68 117.38 -51.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}=\dfrac{1}{2}g_{6}^{2}I.

Only the first six diagrams scale with
q
or
p
due to the closed flavour loops. The remaining diagrams have no such loops because of the vertices or because they are non-planar. Summing these and using the renormalisation scheme discussed above in the chiral Gross-Neveu model results in the beta-functions

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \beta(g_{5})&=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\left[g_{1}g_{5}+g_{5}^{2}(N+p)+g_{7}^{2}p\right]\\
\beta(g_{6})&=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\left[g_{3}g_{6}+g_{6}^{2}p+g_{7}^{2}(N+p)\right]\\
\beta(g_{7})&=-\dfrac{1}{2\pi}\left[g_{1}g_{7}+g_{3}g_{7}+g_{5}g_{7}(N+p)+g_{6}g_{7}p\right]\end{aligned}
